People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… it doesn’t really feel like me, doctor. I mean, I know I’m the one doing it, and I don’t want to make a big fuss about it, but it feels odd after. Like at church, I’ll be by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up and just not moving, or I’ll keep folding the same napkins while people are waiting.

That’s why I came in, really. It embarrasses me some, because I’m not trying to be difficult or strange. It just happens, and then I feel like… well, that wasn’t how I meant to be. Does that make sense?

Or I’ll fold the same napkins over and over while folks are waiting on me. I don’t mean to cause trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing these actions, like folding the napkins, over and over even when you're not at church?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s at church, doctor. It just sort of happens there, and I know it looks strange.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like doing these things, like folding the napkins over and over, is causing you any distress?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it distress, doctor. It’s more embarrassing when someone points it out or looks at me. I don’t want to bother anyone. Mostly I just think, “Oh, I should stop now,” and I try to act normal. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and make you feel like you need to do these actions, like folding the napkins?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so, doctor. Not like a thought telling me I have to do it. It’s more like I just find myself doing it, and then I notice people waiting or looking. I’m sorry if that’s not a very clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel nervous or anxious when you're around other people, especially in situations like the ones at church?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, doctor… I don’t think I’m especially nervous around people. Maybe a little uncomfortable if I notice they’re watching me, like at church, but I try not to make a fuss about it. I don’t know if that counts as anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about what other people think of you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I worry about it all the time, doctor. I mean… I don’t want people thinking badly of me, of course. But I try to behave right and not be a bother. If I notice someone looking, I might feel a little ashamed, but I wouldn’t say I sit and worry over it a lot. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions, like folding the napkins, a specific number of times?\n\nPatient: Not a certain number, no… I don’t count it like that. It’s just, well, at church I’ll get caught doing odd things, like folding the same napkins again and again while people are waiting, or I’ve stood there by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up and not really moving. I know it must look strange. I’m not trying to make trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must perform these actions, like folding the napkins, in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it has to be a very special way, no. I mean… I might fuss with it a little, trying to make it look right, but I don’t have a rule in my head about it. Sometimes I just get stuck doing it, I suppose. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any unwanted thoughts that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, doctor… not like thoughts that keep pushing in over and over. I mean, I have ordinary worries sometimes, same as anyone, but I can’t say there’s one thought that keeps coming back and bothering me. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an overwhelming urge to perform these repetitive actions, like folding the napkins?\n\nPatient: Overwhelming… I don’t know if I’d call it that, doctor. It’s more like I get sort of caught there. Like at church, I’ll be by the potluck table and I’ll just keep folding the same napkins while people are waiting, or I’ll be holding the serving spoon up and not moving like I should. I don’t mean to do it, and I know it must look odd.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are doing these repetitive actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so, doctor. I’m not doing it because I think something awful will happen if I don’t. It’s more… I just get stuck in it for a bit, or I don’t move on like I should. I know that probably sounds strange, but I’m not trying to stop anything bad, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions, such as folding the napkins, are part of a routine or ritual that you feel you need to complete?\n\nPatient: No, doctor, I don’t think it’s a routine or a ritual exactly. It’s not like I’m trying to finish it a certain way. I just… I get stuck doing something odd, like folding that same stack of napkins over and over at the potluck, or standing there holding the serving spoon up and not moving while people are waiting. I know it doesn’t look right, but I’m not meaning for it to be some special thing I have to complete.\n\nDoctor: Do these behaviors, like folding the napkins or standing still with the serving spoon, interfere with your ability to participate in church activities or affect your relationships with others there?\n\nPatient: Well… a little, I suppose. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because the church folks are kind to me. But I have had to step back from some choir things and helping with church volunteering.\n\nSometimes I mix up the plans, or I don’t handle what I was supposed to, and then someone else has to redo it. I think it makes people uncomfortable sometimes, too. I feel bad about that. I hope that makes sense, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these behaviors, such as folding the napkins or standing still with the serving spoon, are a part of who you are or something that doesn't quite feel like you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… it doesn’t really feel like me, doctor. I mean, I know I’m the one doing it, and I don’t want to make a big fuss about it, but it feels odd after. Like at church, I’ll be by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up and just not moving, or I’ll keep folding the same napkins while people are waiting.\n\nThat’s why I came in, really. It embarrasses me some, because I’m not trying to be difficult or strange. It just happens, and then I feel like… well, that wasn’t how I meant to be. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been a bit of a perfectionist or had habits like this, or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I was always like this, no. I mean, I’ve always tried to do things right, especially at church, but this particular sort of thing… it’s more recent.\n\nIt seemed to start after my closest choir friend passed away, about… 11 months ago now. I don’t want to blame everything on that, but that’s when I began noticing these odd moments more. Is that the kind of timing you mean, doctor?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Maybe a little uncomfortable if I notice they’re watching me, like at church, but I try not to make a fuss about it. I don’t know if that counts as anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about what other people think of you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I worry about it all the time, doctor. I mean… I don’t want people thinking badly of me, of course. But I try to behave right and not be a bother. If I notice someone looking, I might feel a little ashamed, but I wouldn’t say I sit and worry over it a lot. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions, like folding the napkins, a specific number of times?\n\nPatient: Not a certain number, no… I don’t count it like that. It’s just, well, at church I’ll get caught doing odd things, like folding the same napkins again and again while people are waiting, or I’ve stood there by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up and not really moving. I know it must look strange. I’m not trying to make trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must perform these actions, like folding the napkins, in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it has to be a very special way, no. I mean… I might fuss with it a little, trying to make it look right, but I don’t have a rule in my head about it. Sometimes I just get stuck doing it, I suppose. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any unwanted thoughts that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, doctor… not like thoughts that keep pushing in over and over. I mean, I have ordinary worries sometimes, same as anyone, but I can’t say there’s one thought that keeps coming back and bothering me. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an overwhelming urge to perform these repetitive actions, like folding the napkins?\n\nPatient: Overwhelming… I don’t know if I’d call it that, doctor. It’s more like I get sort of caught there. Like at church, I’ll be by the potluck table and I’ll just keep folding the same napkins while people are waiting, or I’ll be holding the serving spoon up and not moving like I should. I don’t mean to do it, and I know it must look odd.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are doing these repetitive actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so, doctor. I’m not doing it because I think something awful will happen if I don’t. It’s more… I just get stuck in it for a bit, or I don’t move on like I should. I know that probably sounds strange, but I’m not trying to stop anything bad, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions, such as folding the napkins, are part of a routine or ritual that you feel you need to complete?\n\nPatient: No, doctor, I don’t think it’s a routine or a ritual exactly. It’s not like I’m trying to finish it a certain way. I just… I get stuck doing something odd, like folding that same stack of napkins over and over at the potluck, or standing there holding the serving spoon up and not moving while people are waiting. I know it doesn’t look right, but I’m not meaning for it to be some special thing I have to complete.\n\nDoctor: Do these behaviors, like folding the napkins or standing still with the serving spoon, interfere with your ability to participate in church activities or affect your relationships with others there?\n\nPatient: Well… a little, I suppose. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because the church folks are kind to me. But I have had to step back from some choir things and helping with church volunteering.\n\nSometimes I mix up the plans, or I don’t handle what I was supposed to, and then someone else has to redo it. I think it makes people uncomfortable sometimes, too. I feel bad about that. I hope that makes sense, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these behaviors, such as folding the napkins or standing still with the serving spoon, are a part of who you are or something that doesn't quite feel like you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… it doesn’t really feel like me, doctor. I mean, I know I’m the one doing it, and I don’t want to make a big fuss about it, but it feels odd after. Like at church, I’ll be by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up and just not moving, or I’ll keep folding the same napkins while people are waiting.\n\nThat’s why I came in, really. It embarrasses me some, because I’m not trying to be difficult or strange. It just happens, and then I feel like… well, that wasn’t how I meant to be. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been a bit of a perfectionist or had habits like this, or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I was always like this, no. I mean, I’ve always tried to do things right, especially at church, but this particular sort of thing… it’s more recent.\n\nIt seemed to start after my closest choir friend passed away, about… 11 months ago now. I don’t want to blame everything on that, but that’s when I began noticing these odd moments more. Is that the kind of timing you mean, doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these behaviors are something you want to do, or do they feel alien to you?\n\nPatient: No, doctor… I don’t feel like I want to do it, not really. It feels strange to me, like I sort of get stuck.\n\nI came because at church I keep doing these odd little things—standing by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up and not moving, or folding the same stack of napkins over and over while people are waiting on me. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any unwanted thoughts that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, doctor… not like thoughts that keep pushing in over and over. I mean, I have ordinary worries sometimes, same as anyone, but I can’t say there’s one thought that keeps coming back and bothering me. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an overwhelming urge to perform these repetitive actions, like folding the napkins?\n\nPatient: Overwhelming… I don’t know if I’d call it that, doctor. It’s more like I get sort of caught there. Like at church, I’ll be by the potluck table and I’ll just keep folding the same napkins while people are waiting, or I’ll be holding the serving spoon up and not moving like I should. I don’t mean to do it, and I know it must look odd.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are doing these repetitive actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so, doctor. I’m not doing it because I think something awful will happen if I don’t. It’s more… I just get stuck in it for a bit, or I don’t move on like I should. I know that probably sounds strange, but I’m not trying to stop anything bad, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions, such as folding the napkins, are part of a routine or ritual that you feel you need to complete?\n\nPatient: No, doctor, I don’t think it’s a routine or a ritual exactly. I just… I get stuck doing something odd, like folding that same stack of napkins over and over at the potluck, or standing there holding the serving spoon up and not moving while people are waiting. I don’t mean to do it, and I know it must look odd.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are doing these repetitive actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so, doctor. I’m not doing it because I think something awful will happen if I don’t. It’s more… I just get stuck in it for a bit, or I don’t move on like I should. I know that probably sounds strange, but I’m not trying to stop anything bad, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions, such as folding the napkins, are part of a routine or ritual that you feel you need to complete?\n\nPatient: No, doctor, I don’t think it’s a routine or a ritual exactly. It’s not like I’m trying to finish it a certain way. I just… I get stuck doing something odd, like folding that same stack of napkins over and over at the potluck, or standing there holding the serving spoon up and not moving while people are waiting. I know it doesn’t look right, but I’m not meaning for it to be some special thing I have to complete.\n\nDoctor: Do these behaviors, like folding the napkins or standing still with the serving spoon, interfere with your ability to participate in church activities or affect your relationships with others there?\n\nPatient: Well… a little, I suppose. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because the church folks are kind to me. But I have had to step back from some choir things and helping with church volunteering.\n\nSometimes I mix up the plans, or I don’t handle what I was supposed to, and then someone else has to redo it. I think it makes people uncomfortable sometimes, too. I feel bad about that. I hope that makes sense, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these behaviors, such as folding the napkins or standing still with the serving spoon, are a part of who you are or something that doesn't quite feel like you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… it doesn’t really feel like me, doctor. I mean, I know I’m the one doing it, and I don’t want to make a big fuss about it, but it feels odd after. Like at church, I’ll be by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up and just not moving, or I’ll keep folding the same napkins while people are waiting.\n\nThat’s why I came in, really. It embarrasses me some, because I’m not trying to be difficult or strange. It just happens, and then I feel like… well, that wasn’t how I meant to be. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been a bit of a perfectionist or had habits like this, or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I was always like this, no. I mean, I’ve always tried to do things right, especially at church, but this particular sort of thing… it’s more recent.\n\nIt seemed to start after my closest choir friend passed away, about… 11 months ago now. I don’t want to blame everything on that, but that’s when I began noticing these odd moments more. Is that the kind of timing you mean, doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these behaviors are something you want to do, or do they feel alien to you?\n\nPatient: No, doctor… I don’t feel like I want to do it, not really. It feels strange to me, like I sort of get stuck.\n\nI came because at church I keep doing these odd little things—standing by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up and not moving, or folding the same stack of napkins over and over while people are waiting on me. I know it sounds peculiar, and I’m sorry if I’m not explaining it right, but it doesn’t feel like something I choose on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally had difficulty adapting to social situations or following societal norms throughout your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been throughout my whole life, doctor. It’s more like I get sort of caught there. I don’t mean to do it, and I know it must look odd.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are doing these repetitive actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so, doctor. Maybe a little uncomfortable if I notice they’re watching me, like at church, but I try not to make a fuss about it. I don’t know if that counts as anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about what other people think of you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I worry about it all the time, doctor. I mean… I don’t want people thinking badly of me, of course. But I try to behave right and not be a bother. If I notice someone looking, I might feel a little ashamed, but I wouldn’t say I sit and worry over it a lot. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions, like folding the napkins, a specific number of times?\n\nPatient: Not a certain number, no… I don’t count it like that. It’s just, well, at church I’ll get caught doing odd things, like folding the same napkins again and again while people are waiting, or I’ve stood there by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up and not really moving. I know it must look strange. I’m not trying to make trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must perform these actions, like folding the napkins, in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it has to be a very special way, no. I mean… I might fuss with it a little, trying to make it look right, but I don’t have a rule in my head about it. Sometimes I just get stuck doing it, I suppose. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any unwanted thoughts that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, doctor… not like thoughts that keep pushing in over and over. I mean, I have ordinary worries sometimes, same as anyone, but I can’t say there’s one thought that keeps coming back and bothering me. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an overwhelming urge to perform these repetitive actions, like folding the napkins?\n\nPatient: Overwhelming… I don’t know if I’d call it that, doctor. It’s more like I get sort of caught there. Like at church, I’ll be by the potluck table and I’ll just keep folding the same napkins while people are waiting, or I’ll be holding the serving spoon up and not moving like I should. I don’t mean to do it, and I know it must look odd.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are doing these repetitive actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so, doctor. I’m not doing it because I think something awful will happen if I don’t. It’s more… I just get stuck in it for a bit, or I don’t move on like I should. I know that probably sounds strange, but I’m not trying to stop anything bad, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these actions, such as folding the napkins, are part of a routine or ritual that you feel you need to complete?\n\nPatient: No, doctor, I don’t think it’s a routine or a ritual exactly. It’s not like I’m trying to finish it a certain way. I just… I get stuck doing something odd, like folding that same stack of napkins over and over at the potluck, or standing there holding the serving spoon up and not moving while people are waiting. I know it doesn’t look right, but I’m not meaning for it to be some special thing I have to complete.\n\nDoctor: Do these behaviors, like folding the napkins or standing still with the serving spoon, interfere with your ability to participate in church activities or affect your relationships with others there?\n\nPatient: Well… a little, I suppose. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because the church folks are kind to me. But I have had to step back from some choir things and helping with church volunteering.\n\nSometimes I mix up the plans, or I don’t handle what I was supposed to, and then someone else has to redo it. I think it makes people uncomfortable sometimes, too. I feel bad about that. I hope that makes sense, doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these behaviors, such as folding the napkins or standing still with the serving spoon, are a part of who you are or something that doesn't quite feel like you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… it doesn’t really feel like me, doctor. I mean, I know I’m the one doing it, and I don’t want to make a big fuss about it, but it feels odd after. Like at church, I’ll be by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up and just not moving, or I’ll keep folding the same napkins while people are waiting.\n\nThat’s why I came in, really. It embarrasses me some, because I’m not trying to be difficult or strange. It just happens, and then I feel like… well, that wasn’t how I meant to be. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been a bit of a perfectionist or had habits like this, or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I was always like this, no. I mean, I’ve always tried to do things right, especially at church, but this particular sort of thing… it’s more recent.\n\nIt seemed to start after my closest choir friend passed away, about… 11 months ago now. I don’t want to blame everything on that, but that’s when I began noticing these odd moments more. Is that the kind of timing you mean, doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these behaviors are something you want to do, or do they feel alien to you?\n\nPatient: No, doctor… I don’t feel like I want to do it, not really. It feels strange to me, like I sort of get stuck.\n\nI came because at church I keep doing these odd little things—standing by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up and not moving, or folding the same stack of napkins over and over while people are waiting on me. I know it sounds peculiar, and I’m sorry if I’m not explaining it right, but it doesn’t feel like something I choose on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally had difficulty adapting to social situations or following societal norms throughout your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s been throughout my whole life, doctor. I mean, I’ve managed well enough with people, I think.\n\nThis change… it’s really been since my closest choir friend died, about 11 months ago. That’s when things started feeling harder or different. I hope that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have these behaviors, like folding the napkins or standing still with the serving spoon, significantly impacted your ability to interact or get along with others at church or in other social settings?\n\nPatient: Well… a little, yes. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m causing big trouble, because everyone at church has been very kind.\n\nBut I have had to step back from some of the choir things and some volunteering. I’ll mix up plans sometimes, or I don’t quite do what I was supposed to handle, and then someone has to fix it or redo it. And I can tell people get uncomfortable, even if they’re polite about it.\n\nI feel bad about that. I hope I’m answering that right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these behaviors are a part of yourself or something that is foreign to you?\n\nPatient: It feels more foreign to me, doctor. I don’t like to think of it as “me,” exactly.\n\nI mean, I’m just trying to help at church, and then I’ll find myself doing something odd—like standing there by the potluck table with the serving spoon up and not moving, or folding the same napkins again and again while people are waiting.
